```text
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
10	
11	\looseness=-1
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QXVUKELoLMZ2TnhTWGrpyq`</summary>

```text
42	\label{sec:injection}
43	
44	We use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic, diagnosing that items function differently without attributing cause (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
45	
46	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a stylized uniform injection pattern, reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power; realistic contamination (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) would produce weaker signals.
```

</details>

The cluster bootstrap 95\\% CI ($[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$)---a family-level resampling method independent of DEFF methodology---excludes the weighted-mean estimate (13.5\\%), providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound.         "oldStart": 41,

        "newStart": 41,

        "lines": [
          " \\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}",
          " \\label{sec:injection}",
          " ",
          "-We use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic, diagnosing that items function differently without attributing cause (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.",
          "+We use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic and flags items that function differently without attributing cause (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a stylized uniform injection pattern, reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power; realistic contamination (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) would produce weaker signals.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{10\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations: DIF captures a broader signal---including capability evolution and training methodology shifts---of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs. The base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former; either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate ($K{=}3$ for smaller corpora; \\Cref{tab:k3_power}), triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline (C\\% $\\approx 12\\%$ for MMLU); (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring (DIF alone cannot determine causation); base/instruct reversal motivates refined cohort definitions; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags identify items warranting expert review, not automatic removal---DIF is necessary but not sufficient for item bias~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}), or apply equating adjustments~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains,suk2026rethinking}.\n",

85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-2
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal; \Cref{app:fig_downstream,app:fig_rho_degradation}). Among the 200 most displaced models, 33.6\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$). DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with a cohort-differential of $0.29$\,pp ($d = 0.31$; individual displacements reach ${\pm}800$; \Cref{app:score_inflation}).
91	
92	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
93	\label{sec:gsm8k}
94	
95	\looseness=-2
96	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
97	
98	\begin{table}[t]
99	\centering
100	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\rho$/$\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio = C\%/Rand.\ C\%, with baseline varying by benchmark. All six show substantial DIF (45--95$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}
101	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
102	\small
103	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
104	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}
105	\toprule
106	Benchmark & Items & C\% & $\rho$ & $\tau$ & Flip\% & Rand.\ C\% & Ratio \\
107	\midrule
108	MMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\times$ \\
109	WinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\times$ \\
110	TruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\times$ \\
111	ARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\times$ \\
112	GSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\times$ \\
113	HellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\times$ \\
114	\bottomrule
115	\end{tabular}}
116	\end{table}
117	
118	Semi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).

All six show substantial DIF (45--95$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).\n",

```text
1	\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
13	
14	\looseness=-2
15	Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations: DIF captures a broader signal---including capability evolution and training methodology shifts---of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs. The base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former; either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-2
21	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\geq}$500 models accumulate ($K{=}3$ for smaller corpora; \Cref{tab:k3_power}), triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline (C\% $\approx 12\%$ for MMLU); (2)~\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring (DIF alone cannot determine causation); base/instruct reversal motivates refined cohort definitions; (3)~\emph{Correct}: DIF flags identify items warranting expert review, not automatic removal---DIF is necessary but not sufficient for item bias~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\Cref{app:score_inflation}), or apply equating adjustments~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains,suk2026rethinking}.
22	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{10\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations: DIF captures a broader signal---including capability evolution and training methodology shifts---of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs. The base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former; either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate ($K{=}3$ for smaller corpora; \\Cref{tab:k3_power}), triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline (C\\% $\\approx 12\\%$ for MMLU); (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring (DIF alone cannot determine causation); base/instruct reversal motivates refined cohort definitions; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags identify items warranting expert review, not automatic removal---DIF is necessary but not sufficient for item bias~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}), or apply equating adjustments~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains,suk2026rethinking}.\n",

          " \\looseness=-2",
          "-Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations: DIF captures a broader signal---including capability evolution and training methodology shifts---of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs. The base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former; either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.",
          "+Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.",

Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). 1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3

5	Across six METABENCH benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts (29.2\% for MMLU), while aggregate rankings remain preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap labels, a proxy for contamination (Jaccard $= 0.206$); opposite base-vs-instruct patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$) confirm that DIF captures signal beyond contamination.

1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Figure A3: MF2 ρ degradation — DIF-ordered vs random item removal."""
3	
4	import matplotlib
5	matplotlib.use('Agg')
6	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
7	import pandas as pd
8	import numpy as np
9	import os
10	
11	plt.rcParams.update({
12	    'font.family': 'DejaVu Serif',
13	    'font.size': 9,
14	    'axes.titlesize': 10,
15	    'axes.labelsize': 9,
16	    'xtick.labelsize': 8,
17	    'ytick.labelsize': 8,
18	    'legend.fontsize': 8,
19	    'figure.dpi': 300,
20	    'savefig.dpi': 300,
21	    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',
22	    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,
23	    'axes.spines.top': False,
24	    'axes.spines.right': False,
25	    'lines.linewidth': 1.5,
26	    'pdf.fonttype': 42,
27	    'ps.fonttype': 42,
28	})
29	
30	COL_DIF = '#D55E00'
31	COL_RND = '#0072B2'
32	
33	DATA_PATH = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/artifacts/mf2_rho_degradation/degradation_curves.csv'
34	OUT_DIR = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/figures/paper'
35	
36	df = pd.read_csv(DATA_PATH)
37	
38	# Total item counts (back-calculated from 5% removal row)
39	bench_items = {}
40	for b in df['benchmark'].unique():
41	    n_at_5 = df[(df['benchmark'] == b) & (df['removal_pct'] == 5) & (df['removal_type'] == 'dif_ordered')]['n_items_remaining'].values[0]
42	    bench_items[b] = round(n_at_5 / 0.95)
43	
44	BENCH_ORDER = ['truthfulqa', 'arc', 'winogrande', 'gsm8k', 'hellaswag', 'mmlu']
45	BENCH_LABELS = {
46	    'truthfulqa': 'TruthfulQA',
47	    'arc': 'ARC',
48	    'winogrande': 'WinoGrande',
49	    'gsm8k': 'GSM8K',
50	    'hellaswag': 'HellaSwag',
51	    'mmlu': 'MMLU',
52	}
53	
54	fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 3, figsize=(7, 4.5))
55	axes = axes.flatten()
56	
57	for idx, bench in enumerate(BENCH_ORDER):
58	    ax = axes[idx]
59	    bdf = df[df['benchmark'] == bench]
60	
61	    dif = bdf[bdf['removal_type'] == 'dif_ordered'].sort_values('removal_pct')
62	    rnd_mean = bdf[bdf['removal_type'] == 'random_mean'].sort_values('removal_pct')
63	    rnd_std = bdf[bdf['removal_type'] == 'random_std'].sort_values('removal_pct')
64	
65	    pcts = dif['removal_pct'].values
66	    y_dif = dif['spearman_rho'].values
67	    y_rnd = rnd_mean['spearman_rho'].values
68	    y_std = rnd_std['spearman_rho'].values
69	
70	    ax.fill_between(pcts, y_rnd - y_std, y_rnd + y_std,
71	                    color=COL_RND, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0)
72	    ax.plot(pcts, y_dif, color=COL_DIF, marker='o', markersize=3.5,
73	            label='DIF-ordered', zorder=3)
74	    ax.plot(pcts, y_rnd, color=COL_RND, marker='s', markersize=3.5,
75	            linestyle='--', label='Random', zorder=3)
76	
77	    # Threshold lines
78	    for xv, lbl in [(15, 'Monitor/Flag'), (25, 'Flag/Act')]:
79	        ax.axvline(xv, color='#888888', linewidth=0.7, linestyle=':', zorder=1)
80	
81	    # Y-axis: adaptive per panel with some padding
82	    all_y = np.concatenate([y_dif, y_rnd - y_std, y_rnd + y_std])
83	    ymin, ymax = all_y.min(), all_y.max()
84	    margin = (ymax - ymin) * 0.15
85	    ax.set_ylim(ymin - margin, ymax + margin * 2)  # extra top margin for labels
86	
87	    # Re-draw threshold text after ylim is set
88	    for txt in ax.texts:
89	        txt.remove()
90	    ytop = ax.get_ylim()[1]
91	    for xv, lbl in [(15, 'Monitor/Flag'), (25, 'Flag/Act')]:
92	        ax.text(xv, ytop, lbl, ha='center', va='bottom',
93	                fontsize=5.5, color='#666666', clip_on=False)
94	
95	    n_total = bench_items[bench]
96	    ax.set_title(f'{BENCH_LABELS[bench]} ($N$={n_total:,})')
97	
98	    ax.set_xticks([5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, 35, 40, 50])
99	    ax.tick_params(axis='both', which='both', length=3)
100	
101	    if idx >= 3:
102	        ax.set_xlabel('Items removed (%)')
103	    if idx % 3 == 0:
104	        ax.set_ylabel('Spearman $\\rho$')
105	
106	    # Legend only in first panel
107	    if idx == 0:
108	        ax.legend(loc='lower left', frameon=False, handlelength=1.8)
109	
110	fig.tight_layout(h_pad=1.0, w_pad=0.8)
111	
112	os.makedirs(OUT_DIR, exist_ok=True)
113	fig.savefig(os.path.join(OUT_DIR, 'fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf'))
114	fig.savefig(os.path.join(OUT_DIR, 'fig_A3_rho_degradation.png'))
115	print('Saved fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf and .png')
116

89	\looseness=-2
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average (\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).
91	
92	\begin{figure}[t]
93	\centering
94	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
95	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds from \Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}
96	\label{fig:rho_degradation}
97	\end{figure}
98	
99	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
100	\label{sec:gsm8k}

- Page 8: Table 3 at top, then all text sections (§4.6, §4.7, §5 Discussion, §5.1, §5.2, §6 Conclusion) — Conclusion is fully on page 8! 


- Page 9: Figure 3 at the top spanning both columns, then Limitations, then References

This layout keeps the Conclusion on page 8, but now I'm realizing there's a potential issue — Figure 3 is referenced in §4.6 on page 8 but doesn't appear until page 9, which is acceptable for floating figures, though I need to verify whether having Figure 3 on page 9 would count as main text content and violate the page limit.

Looking at EMNLP's rules, main text is capped at 8 pages while Limitations and references don't count. The tricky part is that Figure 3 belongs to the experiments section, so if it appears on page 9, it might technically be main text content exceeding the limit. But examining the actual page 9 layout more carefully, the Limitations section continues below the figure, which is fine since Limitations doesn't count toward the page limit. The real question is whether Figure 3 itself, as part of the experimental content, being on page 9 violates the constraint.

I'm considering a few options to move the figure earlier: I could use `figure*[t]` to force it to the top of an earlier page, try `figure*[b]` to place it at the bottom of page 8, or shift the figure code earlier in the source so LaTeX positions it sooner. The key is understanding how LaTeX's float placement works with the `[t]` option. placed it in §4.6 (which is on page 8), LaTeX puts it at the top of page 9 because there's not enough room on page 8.

If I move the `figure*` code to appear before §4.4 or §4.5 in the source, LaTeX might float it to the top of page 7 or 8 instead.

Let me try moving the figure* to appear right after §4.3 (comparison with web-overlap labels), before §4.4 (structural analysis). This way LaTeX would try to place it at the top of page 7 or 8.

Wait, actually, `figure*[t]` in a two-column layout goes to the top of the next page from where it's defined. If I put it just before §4.4, LaTeX might place it at the top of page 7 or 8.

Looking back at the actual page 8 layout, I see Table 3 and sections 4.6-4.7 on the left column, with section 5 continuing on the right and the conclusion fully fitting on page 8. The figure floated to page 9, which raises the question of whether that violates the page limit—EMNLP allows 8 pages for the main content, so I need to clarify if a floating figure on page 9 counts against that.

In practice, reviewers typically don't flag this as a violation if the text itself fits in 8 pages, but it's risky. A safer approach would be to either make Figure 3 single-column with larger, more readable panels, or try to force it onto page 7 or 8 by moving its definition earlier in the source file.

55	
56	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
57	\label{sec:structural}
58	
59	\looseness=-1
60	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
61	
62	\begin{table}[t]
63	\centering
64	\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\% below 31\%; $\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}
65	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
66	\small
67	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
68	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
69	\toprule
70	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
71	\midrule
72	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
73	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
74	$\theta$-cond.\ (sens.) & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
75	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
76	\bottomrule
77	\end{tabular}}
78	\end{table}
79	
80	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
81	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
82	
83	\looseness=-1
84	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with reference-case detection power of $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; under stylized uniform injection; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average (\Cref{app:fig_matching}). The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} \approx 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.069$ at $k{=}10$ (18\% of domains) and $0.167$ at $k{=}20$; at $k \geq 30$ (${>}50\%$ of domains), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ exceeds 0.36 (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Even at $k{=}20$, inflation (${\sim}$17~pp) cannot fully account for the observed 31\% C\% rate, which exceeds the random baseline by ${>}50\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ exceeds the weighted-mean lower bound (13.5\%), underscoring $k \leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\sim}$20\% for heterogeneous contamination; the monotonic dose-response (\Cref{fig:dose_response}) confirms detection power scales continuously with effect magnitude (\Cref{app:method_details}).
85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-2
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average (\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).
91	
92	\begin{figure*}[t]
93	\centering
94	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
95	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds from \Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}
96	\label{fig:rho_degradation}
97	\end{figure*}
98	
99	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
100	\label{sec:gsm8k}
101	
102	\looseness=-2
103	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
104	
```

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \\geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\\sim}$29\\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[29.2\\%, 37.8\\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$, upper bound driven by mega-family over-representation; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two design-effect (DEFF) estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\\% and weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5\\%, bracketing temporal C\\% at 13.5--29.2\\%. The cluster bootstrap 95\\% CI ($[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$)---a family-level resampling method independent of DEFF methodology---excludes the weighted-mean estimate (13.5\\%), providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound. Under weighted-mean DEFF (C\\% $= 13.5\\%$), all downstream conclusions hold: $\\rho = 0.999$, score shift $= {+}1.77$\\,pp (\\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Under 100 random permutations within the 2023 cohort (540 vs.\\ 540), the 99th percentile of item-level $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$ is 1.45---just below the ETS~C threshold of 1.5---confirming that the threshold maintains a per-item false-positive rate below 1\\%; together with the Monte Carlo power analysis (TPR $\\geq 0.68$; \\Cref{sec:validation}), this validates ETS~C classification for the LLM setting. No non-dominant family drives the signal (LOFO $|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp for families ${\\leq}15\\%$ of cohort; dominant-family drops reflect composition, not signal; \\Cref{tab:lofo}); graduated de-duplication confirms C\\% scales with power, not redundancy (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\\% of 0.62\\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$; at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,090 / ${\\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$; $p = 0.88$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Crucially, label-independent evidence confirms mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$; \\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{injected}} - \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \\Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\n\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\%) thresholds from \\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}\n\\label{fig:rho_degradation}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\\% converges to ${\\sim}$28--29\\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\\% below 31\\%; $\\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ C\\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond.\\ (sens.) & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with reference-case detection power of $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; under stylized uniform injection; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.069$ at $k{=}10$ (18\\% of domains) and $0.167$ at $k{=}20$; at $k \\geq 30$ (${>}50\\%$ of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ exceeds 0.36 (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Even at $k{=}20$, inflation (${\\sim}$17~pp) cannot fully account for the observed 31\\% C\\% rate, which exceeds the random baseline by ${>}50\\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ exceeds the weighted-mean lower bound (13.5\\%), underscoring $k \\leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% for heterogeneous contamination; the monotonic dose-response (\\Cref{fig:dose_response}) confirms detection power scales continuously with effect magnitude (\\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\\rho = 0.997$, mean $\\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\\leq 2.9\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average (\\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). $\\rho$/$\\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\\%/random baseline.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).\n",
        "numLines": 126,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 126
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nAcross six benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts (29.2\\% for MMLU) while aggregate rankings remain preserved ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); opposite base-vs-instruct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$) confirm that DIF captures signal beyond contamination.\n",
        "numLines": 6,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 6
      }

{
  "parentUuid": "33b898f5-b087-4729-b9d0-cfc6bac667b1",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\enlargethispage{10\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",
        "numLines": 22,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 22
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/discussion.tex"
  },

56	\begin{figure*}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
59	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds from \Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}
60	\label{fig:rho_degradation}

62	
63	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
64	\label{sec:structural}
65	
66	\looseness=-1
67	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
68	
69	\begin{table}[t]
70	\centering
71	\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\% below 31\%; $\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}
72	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
73	\small
74	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
75	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
76	\toprule
77	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
78	\midrule
79	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
80	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
81	$\theta$-cond.\ (sens.) & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
82	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
83	\bottomrule
84	\end{tabular}}
85	\end{table}
86	
87	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
88	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
89	
90	\looseness=-1
91	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with reference-case detection power of $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; under stylized uniform injection; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average (\Cref{app:fig_matching}). The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} \approx 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.069$ at $k{=}10$ (18\% of domains) and $0.167$ at $k{=}20$; at $k \geq 30$ (${>}50\%$ of domains), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ exceeds 0.36 (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Even at $k{=}20$, inflation (${\sim}$17~pp) cannot fully account for the observed 31\% C\% rate, which exceeds the random baseline by ${>}50\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ exceeds the weighted-mean lower bound (13.5\%), underscoring $k \leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\sim}$20\% for heterogeneous contamination; the monotonic dose-response (\Cref{fig:dose_response}) confirms detection power scales continuously with effect magnitude (\Cref{app:method_details}).
92	
93	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
94	\label{sec:downstream}
95	
96	\looseness=-2
97	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average (\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).
98	
99	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
100	\label{sec:gsm8k}
101	
102	\looseness=-2
103	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
104	
105	\begin{table}[t]
106	\centering
107	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\ 2024 cohorts). $\rho$/$\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\%/random baseline.}
108	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
109	\small
110	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
111	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}

114	\midrule
115	MMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\times$ \\
116	WinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\times$ \\
117	TruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\times$ \\
118	ARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\times$ \\

0	\section{Experiments}
1	\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
4	
5	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
6	\label{sec:validation}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	A Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
10	
11	\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\sim}$29\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}
12	\label{sec:temporal}
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\% CI: $[29.2\%, 37.8\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$, upper bound driven by mega-family over-representation; \Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two design-effect (DEFF) estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\% and weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5\%, bracketing temporal C\% at 13.5--29.2\%. The cluster bootstrap 95\% CI ($[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$)---a family-level resampling method independent of DEFF methodology---excludes the weighted-mean estimate (13.5\%), providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound. Under weighted-mean DEFF (C\% $= 13.5\%$), all downstream conclusions hold: $\rho = 0.999$, score shift $= {+}1.77$\,pp (\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Under 100 random permutations within the 2023 cohort (540 vs.\ 540), the 99th percentile of item-level $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$ is 1.45---just below the ETS~C threshold of 1.5---confirming that the threshold maintains a per-item false-positive rate below 1\%; together with the Monte Carlo power analysis (TPR $\geq 0.68$; \Cref{sec:validation}), this validates ETS~C classification for the LLM setting. No non-dominant family drives the signal (LOFO $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp for families ${\leq}15\%$ of cohort; dominant-family drops reflect composition, not signal; \Cref{tab:lofo}); graduated de-duplication confirms C\% scales with power, not redundancy (\Cref{app:dedup_k}).
16	
17	\looseness=-1
18	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\% of 0.62\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$; at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
19	
20	\looseness=-1
21	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. C\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
26	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
27	\small
28	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
29	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
30	\toprule
31	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & C\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\
32	\midrule
33	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
34	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,090 / ${\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
35	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
36	Random split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\
37	Random split 2024 (sanity) & ${\sim}$400 / ${\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\
38	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
39	\bottomrule
40	\end{tabular}}
41	\end{table}
42	
43	\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}
44	\label{sec:groundtruth}
45	
46	\looseness=-2
47	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$; $p = 0.88$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Crucially, label-independent evidence confirms mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\chi^2 = 562.7$; \Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
51	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \mathrm{TPR}_{\text{injected}} - \mathrm{TPR}_{\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
52	\label{fig:dose_response}
53	\end{figure}
54	
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with reference-case detection power of $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; under stylized uniform injection; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.069$ at $k{=}10$ (18\\% of domains) and $0.167$ at $k{=}20$; at $k \\geq 30$ (${>}50\\%$ of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ exceeds 0.36 (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Even at $k{=}20$, inflation (${\\sim}$17~pp) cannot fully account for the observed 31\\% C\\% rate, which exceeds the random baseline by ${>}50\\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ exceeds the weighted-mean lower bound (13.5\\%), underscoring $k \\leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% for heterogeneous contamination; the monotonic dose-response (\\Cref{fig:dose_response}) confirms detection power scales continuously with effect magnitude (\\Cref{app:method_details}).",
  "new_string": "\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \\leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\\% C\\% (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% (\\Cref{app:method_details})."
}

Difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% for heterogeneous contamination; the monotonic dose-response (\\Cref{fig:dose_response}) confirms detection power scales continuously with effect magnitude (\\Cref{app:method_details}).",

The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \\leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\\% C\\% (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.069$ at $k{=}10$ (18\\% of domains) and $0.167$ at $k{=}20$; at $k \\geq 30$ (${>}50\\%$ of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ exceeds 0.36 (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}).     "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. _source line 659 · 2026-05-24T11:14:33.599Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WrVpZq7nCrTdxDWoBkdLmn`</summary>

````text
1	---
2	description: 用 PaperBanana 生成高品质学术概念图（架构/流程/framework）。统计图不要用 PaperBanana，用 draw-paper-figure。需要 GOOGLE_API_KEY。
3	name: paper-banana
4	scope: global
5	tags:
6	  - visualization
7	  - paper
8	  - matplotlib
9	  - llm-codegen
10	  - conceptual-diagram
11	version: 4
12	tools:
13	  - request_secret
14	  - poll_secret_request
15	---
16	
17	# Paper Banana — 高品质学术概念图生成
18	
19	PaperBanana 由 Gemini Pro 自动写 matplotlib 代码，VLM Critic 评估渲染结果并迭代优化，输出顶会级别 300 DPI 概念图。
20	
21	**质量底线：每张图必须达到顶会论文的美观度——高级感、专业感、精致感。绝不接受 matplotlib 默认样式的廉价感。**
22	
23	## 适用范围
24	
25	| 场景 | 用什么 |
26	|------|--------|
27	| **概念图**（架构图、流程图、framework 示意） | **PaperBanana** ← 本 skill |
28	| **统计图**（scatter、bar、line、heatmap、forest） | **draw-paper-figure.skill.md**。**不要用 PaperBanana 画统计图** |
29	| 离线 / 无 GOOGLE_API_KEY | **draw-paper-figure.skill.md** |
30	
31	## 执行环境
32	
33	**PaperBanana 在本机（EC2）运行，不要 SSH 到远程服务器。**
34	
35	EC2（ap-northeast-1 东京）可直接访问 Gemini API，无需代理。
36	
37	## 前置条件
38	
39	### Step 1：获取 GOOGLE_API_KEY
40	
41	```python
42	request_secret(key_id="google_api_key", project="&lt;project_name>", purpose="PaperBanana 概念图生成")
43	# 等待 Director 审批后
44	poll_secret_request(request_id="<返回的 request_id>", project="&lt;project_name>")
45	```
46	
47	如果密钥尚未存储，告知 Director 需要 GOOGLE_API_KEY。
48	
49	### Step 2：安装验证（每次使用前必须检查）
50	
51	```bash
52	# 检查是否已安装
53	paperbanana --version 2>/dev/null || {
54	  echo "PaperBanana 未安装，正在安装..."
55	  pip install paperbanana -q
56	  paperbanana --version
57	}
58	```
59	
60	如果 `paperbanana --version` 失败（命令不存在），必须先 `pip install paperbanana -q` 安装。版本需 ≥ 0.1.2。
61	
62	**设计前必读** `skills/global/paper-visualization.skill.md` 的概念图 L1（防丑 7 条）+ L2（叙事 6 条）+ L3 Pattern + Checklist。统计图部分不适用于 PaperBanana。
63	
64	## 命令：generate
65	
66	概念图用 `generate` 子命令。**必须显式指定 `--vlm-model gemini-2.5-pro`**（默认模型 `gemini-2.0-flash` 已废弃，不指定会崩溃）。**`--iterations 5`**。
67	
68	## 完整流程
69	
70	### Step 1：写意图描述文件
71	
72	将详细设计描述写入文件（太长不适合命令行参数）：
73	
74	```bash
75	cat > /tmp/fig_intent.txt << 'INTENT'
76	Three-panel academic figure for a top-venue paper (full width, aspect ratio ~3.2:1, figsize 14x4.5 inches):
77	
78	Panel (a) 'The Phenomenon' (left, ~38% width):
79	Grouped bar chart. TF bars: dark navy (#2C3E50). AR bars: academic red (#C0392B).
80	Exact data: Base TF=0.501 AR=0.019, ThreadGate TF=0.504 AR=0.064, ...
81	Y-axis 'Mid-turn GAR' range 0–0.55. Only left+bottom spines. Serif font 10pt.
82	Add bracket annotation '8-17x gap' between bar groups.
83	
84	Panel (b) 'Diagnostic Chain' (center, ~24% width):
85	Vertical flowchart, 4 rounded boxes connected by thin gray arrows:
86	  Step 1: light gray (#F5F6F7) box, thin border (#DEE2E6), 'Gate Analysis' + subtitle. Green ✓
87	  Step 2: same style, 'PCGrad Exclusion'. Red ✗
88	  Step 3: same style, 'Representation Check'. Red ✗
89	  Step 4: light green (#D4EDDA) background, 'Conclusion: Exposure bias'. Green ✓
90	No axes, no spines. Clean infographic style.
91	
92	Panel (c) 'Constructive Evidence' (right, ~38% width):
93	Line plot. Data points: (0, 0.064), (3, 0.556), (5, 0.727).
94	Navy line (#2C3E50), open circles (white fill, navy edge, width 2).
95	Light blue fill (#EBF5FB, alpha 0.4) under curve.
96	Annotate '11x recovery' in muted gray, 9pt italic.
97	
98	OVERALL STYLE:
99	- 300 DPI, white background, publication quality
100	- Serif font (Times New Roman or similar) throughout
101	- Panel labels '(a)', '(b)', '(c)' in 11pt bold at top-left
102	- Generous white space (wspace=0.35)
103	- Muted academic palette: navy #2C3E50, red #C0392B, green #27AE60, gray #7F8C8D
104	- NO matplotlib default styling: no gray background, no thick gridlines, no tab10 colors
105	- Drop shadows on boxes: subtle, 2px offset, 10% opacity
106	- Rounded corners 8px on all rectangles
107	- Arrow heads: small, elegant, not bulky
108	INTENT
109	```
110	
111	### Step 2：运行 generate
112	
113	```bash
114	GOOGLE_API_KEY="[REDACTED]" paperbanana generate \
115	  --input /tmp/fig_intent.txt \
116	  --caption "Three-panel diagnostic framework: (a) TF vs AR performance gap, (b) systematic diagnosis chain, (c) beta-steering recovery evidence" \
117	  --vlm-model gemini-2.5-pro \
118	  --output figures/paper/fig1_overview.png \
119	  --iterations 5
120	```
121	
122	### Step 3：视觉检查（不可跳过）
123	
124	**Read 生成的 PNG 文件**，视觉评估以下维度：
125	
126	1. **高级感**：是否有顶会论文的精致感？还是看起来像课程作业？
127	2. **颜色**：是否有语义（不是随机分配）？是否和谐、不刺眼？
128	3. **文字**：缩到 column width 后是否 ≥ 7pt 可读？
129	4. **布局**：留白是否合理？元素是否对齐？
130	5. **细节**：圆角、阴影、箭头是否精致？边框是否统一？
131	
132	**不满意 → 必须修改意图描述文件，重新生成。**
133	
134	### Step 4：转 PDF
135	
136	```bash
137	python3 -c "
138	import matplotlib; matplotlib.use('Agg')
139	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt, matplotlib.image as mpimg
140	img = mpimg.imread('figures/paper/fig1_overview.png')
141	h, w = img.shape[:2]
142	fig = plt.figure(figsize=(w/300, h/300), dpi=300)
143	ax = fig.add_axes([0,0,1,1]); ax.imshow(img); ax.axis('off')
144	plt.savefig('figures/paper/fig1_overview.pdf', bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0)
145	plt.close()
146	"
147	```
148	
149	## 意图描述质量红线
150	
151	Intent 质量直接决定图的质量。必须包含以下 7 项（缺一不可）：
152	
153	1. **空间布局**：每个 panel 的宽度占比（如 38%/24%/38%）
154	2. **组件形状 + 尺寸**：圆角半径 8px、figsize (14, 4.5)
155	3. **颜色 hex 码**：不说"蓝色"，说 "#2C3E50"，绑定语义
156	4. **所有文字内容**：box label、副标题、箭头 annotation
157	5. **尺寸规格**：DPI 300、字号 10-12pt body / 8-9pt labels
158	6. **细节规格**：阴影偏移 2px / 10% opacity、箭头粗细、边框宽度
159	7. **反面约束**："NO matplotlib default styling, NO gray background, NO tab10 colors"
160	
161	### 美学风格关键词（必须在 intent 中出现）
162	
163	```
164	STYLE REQUIREMENTS:
165	- Publication quality for a TOP venue (NeurIPS/ICML/EMNLP)
166	- Elegant, premium, sophisticated — NOT cheap or homework-like
167	- Subtle drop shadows (2px, 10% opacity) on boxes
168	- Rounded corners (8px) on all rectangles
169	- Thin, elegant arrow heads — not bulky matplotlib defaults
170	- Serif font (Times) for labels, sans-serif (Helvetica) for annotations
171	- Muted, harmonious color palette — NO saturated primary colors
172	- Generous white space between elements
173	- Consistent visual weight across all panels
174	```
175	
176	### 反面教材（这样的 intent 一定画不好）
177	
178	- "Draw a pipeline diagram with three stages"
179	- "Architecture diagram for our system"
180	
181	太模糊。LLM 会用 matplotlib 默认样式填充所有你没指定的细节，结果就是廉价感。
182	
183	## 迭代策略（至少 3 轮外部迭代，Hero Figure 至少 5 轮）
184	
185	PaperBanana 内部有 VLM Critic 迭代（`--iterations 5`），但这还不够。你必须做**外部迭代**：
186	
187	### 第 1 轮：基础生成
188	运行 PaperBanana → Read PNG → 识别大方向问题（布局、配色、结构）
189	
190	### 第 2 轮：结构修复
191	修改意图描述（布局占比、颜色 hex、组件关系）→ 重新运行 → Read PNG
192	
193	### 第 3 轮：细节打磨
194	修改意图描述（字号、阴影、对齐、箭头粗细）→ 重新运行 → Read PNG
195	
196	### 第 4 轮（Hero Figure）：风格统一
197	线宽 / 字号 / 间距全图统一？与论文其他图配色一致？→ 重新运行 → Read PNG
198	
199	### 第 5 轮（Hero Figure）：最终 polish
200	像素级检查 → 缩到 column width 后文字 ≥ 7pt？→ 终极判断：放在 NeurIPS best paper 里违和吗？
201	
202	### 判断标准
203	
204	**可以交付**：看起来像 NeurIPS 2025 best paper 里的图。
205	**不可交付**：看起来像 matplotlib gallery 的示例图。
206	
207	## 失败回退
208	
209	如果 PaperBanana 多次崩溃（API 错误、模型不兼容）：
210	
211	1. 确认 `--vlm-model gemini-2.5-pro` 是否指定
212	2. 确认 GOOGLE_API_KEY 是否有效：`curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' "https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta/models?key=$GOOGLE_API_KEY"` 应返回 200
213	3. 如果仍然失败，回退 matplotlib 手写：
214	   - Read `skills/global/paper-visualization.skill.md` 获取 L1-L4 设计标准
215	   - 写 Python 脚本手动画概念图（FancyBboxPatch、FancyArrowPatch 等）
216	   - **同样必须 Read PNG 做视觉检查，至少迭代 3 轮**
217	   - **同样必须达到顶会级别的美观度**
218	   - 保存 .py 脚本到 figures/paper/ 目录便于后续修改
219	
````

gridlines, no tab10 colors\n- Drop shadows on boxes: subtle, 2px offset, 10% opacity\n- Rounded corners 8px on all rectangles\n- Arrow heads: small, elegant, not bulky\nINTENT\n```\n\n### Step 2：运行 mpimg\nimg = mpimg.imread('figures/paper/fig1_overview.png')\nh, w = img.shape[:2]\nfig = plt.figure(figsize=(w/300, h/300), dpi=300)\nax = fig.add_axes([0,0,1,1]); ax.imshow(img); ax.axis('off')\nplt.savefig('figures/paper/fig1_overview.pdf', bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0)\nplt.close()\n\"\n```\n\n## 意图描述质量红线\n\nIntent 质量直接决定图的质量。必须包含以下 7 项（缺一不可）：\n\n1. **空间布局**：每个 panel
